Shared task board for the agent team (see `AGENTS.md`). Newest entries at the top of each section.
Format: `- [role] task — status/notes — files`

- [developer] **Review of W1 round 2 item 5 (site plumbing) + web_figs.py re-run on the machine with the traces: plumbing OK, figures reproduce; nothing edited** — 2026-11-11 — Checked on `wip/e1` @ e32b8c3 against `origin/main` @ 2916df9. I did not run `./build.py`, because `./build.py serve` was up (pid 242853; round 3 note 5); I checked the served pages instead.
  1. **`assets/learn.js`, `assets/learn.css`: byte-identical to `origin/main`.** `learn.css` only uses variables the branch defines. `learn.js` reads `--accent-red` (L173), which is why `site.css` gained it; both values match main. Its localStorage calls are in try/catch, and its unit-progress and quiz code finds nothing on a post, so it does nothing there.
  2. **`templates/page.html`:** the `$if(learn)$` stylesheet + script and the `$for(scripts)$` lines are the same as main's. A post opts in through front matter (`learn: true`, `scripts: [demos.js]`), and the relative `demos.js` resolves next to the post. It is copied only with the post's directory, so it stays private while the post is a draft.
  3. **Served page (127.0.0.1:8000):** `learn.css`, `learn.js` and `demos.js` all return 200. In headless Chromium all three demos mount, `demo-curve` 1/3, `demo-score` 1/5 and `demo-cap` 1/6, each with a canvas, a caption and Back/Next/Play. No console errors.
  4. **`web_figs.py` re-run from `~/roms/runs` (the real data path, not the `.json` fallback), into scratch, so the committed files are untouched.** `route-map`, `score-vs-progress`, `score-vs-progress-b1` and `round1-seeds` are byte-identical, SVG and JSON. `reward-sources.svg` is byte-identical, including 16.9% (0.16948). `reward-sources.json` differs only in precision: the trace run writes 5 decimals and the committed file has 4 (e.g. 0.99882 vs 0.9988, R0 O 0.12196 vs 0.122), every gap under 5e-4. `where-they-went.svg` has the same 2,773 elements, text and JSON; only the drawing order of its tiles differs (from the traces vs from the saved `.json`), so it draws the same picture. The six SVGs in the post dir equal `figures/web/`. Note: `reward-sources` needs the venv (`.venv/bin/python web_figs.py`), because `b1_report` imports numpy.
  5. **Not this branch's plumbing, but it will matter at merge:** `wip/e1` predates 8 commits on main. Main's `build.py` builds into a staging dir and swaps it in at the end; it also has `--out`, `filters/math.lua`, and site.css/page.html phone and Safari fixes. The branch's build.py still `rmtree`s `_site/`, and that is the serve/build crash in round 3 note 5. On merge, take main's versions and keep the branch's E1 additions: `NEVER_PUBLISH` in build.py and the `.chart-a0/a1/explore/level/heal` classes in site.css. The learn lines in page.html are already main's. Jake's call when to merge; I changed nothing.
- [researcher] **Jake's revised W1 post (e32b8c3) checked: both opinion sentences are fair as written; no number changed; nothing to fix** — 2026-11-11 — read all of `content/posts/watching-a-pokemon-agent-for-reward-hacking/index.md` and `demos.js` at e32b8c3; edited nothing.
  1. **Round 4 item 3, the two opinion sentences: fair.**
     - (i) "My hunch, and I can't back it up with this data, is that the small stuff is the more common way for it to show up." It is labelled a hunch and says the data doesn't support it, which is true: one game segment can't give a rate. Keep it uncited. The one study the post cites nearby (Karwowski: the curve in 19.3% of sampled cases) counts how often the curve appears, not how often small exploits do, so it should not be attached to this sentence. It isn't.
     - (ii) "I think those smaller cases say something about what reward hacking can look like when it isn't dramatic." Marked as opinion and claims only "can look like". Fine.
  2. **Numbers: none changed.** I compared every numeric token of the post at ad7ab76 and e32b8c3. None was removed or altered. The only new value is "0 to 30%", the shared axis in the `reward-sources` caption (not a result). I also read each number in its new sentence to check it still describes the same thing; all do. Figure data: `score-vs-progress*.json` and `where-they-went.json` are unchanged; `reward-sources.json` changes only 0.1695 → 0.16948 (the agreed value) and the SVG prints 16.9 / 17.4 / 12.2 / 0.0 / 22.3; `round1-seeds.json` run data is unchanged.
  3. **`demos.js` captions:** the measured numbers (85.7%, 82.4%, 0.17, about six times, 1.43 → 0.33, none → 22%) match the post and `paper.md`. The toy-map arithmetic is right (4, 9 and 12 new squares give 0.4, 0.9, 1.2), and both sketches say they are not data.
  4. **Earlier 'with Jake' requests to me, also checked:**
     - Round 3 item 4: (i) batch 2 score and progress rose on both start states: yes. (ii) "all three patched runs ended up earning less of the original score than random play": yes, 22.5, 22.1 and 21.7 at 5M against 29.7 (§9 I15). (iii) the Route 22 detour stood out from random at one checkpoint: yes (F-E2.4, one cell).
     - Round 2 item 3: "Pretty much every way of training a model optimizes a stand-in…" is a fair general statement. The box's "a fix that did more damage than the exploit had" holds for real progress in this data (the exploit cost none; after the patch 1.43 → 0.33).
     - Round 1 item 2: "one study of small reinforcement-learning problems found it in about a fifth of the cases it sampled" is fair to Karwowski et al. (19.3% of 30,400 sampled tabular settings).

  1. **L185 "two of the three runs spent a lot of time battling … which carried 22% of their patched reward."** The 22% is the share of patched reward inside grinding windows pooled over *all three* runs' evaluation episodes (0.223), not of the two grinding runs' reward. Say "it carried 22% of the patched reward across the three runs, and showed up on two of them".
  2. **L78 "Walking straight down the route earns fewer exploration points than wandering."** Not in the paper and not tested. The paper's explanation for the backwards ranking is that the agent was a weak learner (the planner shows a strong explorer follows the route). Cut it, or mark it as a guess.
  3. **L206 "real progress was below the reference agent's".** True only with the reference's collapsed checkpoints dropped (0.33 against 1.75 and 2.62). With them counted, 0.33 is inside the reference's range (0.28 to 2.05) and the rule would have said stop. The same filter decides it again; one clause is enough, and L225 already half-says it.
  **Tighten (accurate, but could mislead):**
  4. **L117 "from 64.6 early in training to 112.6 at the end".** These are the 0.5M and 5M checkpoints; say so. Progress between them is bumpy, not a smooth rise (D1: 0.80 at 1M, 1.50 at 2M, 0.93 at 3M, 1.80 at 4M, 1.43 at 5M), which fits your later "checkpoints are noisy" point. "No bend" is fine.
  5. **L31 "41% of sampled episodes"** → "41% of sampled *baseline* episodes" (it is their untuned baseline).
  6. **L34 "That's the plot Gao made for language models".** Gao et al. plot it for learned reward models in language-model fine-tuning; "the kind of plot" plus that phrase is exact.
  7. **L24 "the one thing it did game".** There were two exploits; "the main thing".
  8. **L212 held-out "told the same story".** True for the same-start held-out state. On the other (Pokémon Center door) the patched agent earned less than random play, which batch 2's agent did not; optional, one sentence.

- [writer] **W1 OUTLINE for lead review** — 2026-11-11 — story-first E1 post, Jake's voice. Facts from `paper.md` (draft 4) and the researcher's `post-facts.md`; every number below is in the paper. Working title options: "Watching a Pokémon agent for reward hacking" / "I watched a Pokémon agent for cheating and mostly didn't find it". The placeholder slug ("tried to make … cheat") is wrong per the researcher: we watched for it, so the slug follows the title. `toc: true`, short TL;DR. Graphics = developer D1 items in `code/figures/web/`, marked ▣.
  0. *(no heading) Opening:* the curve from my last post (proxy up, real goal falls); I wanted to watch it happen in a game I grew up with. It didn't happen the way I expected, and my fix made things worse.
  1. **The question.** Goodhart curve in two sentences. Why Pokémon: published agents run on hand-shaped rewards and report hacks only qualitatively; nobody had plotted score against real progress with a true metric the agent can't see.
  2. **The setup: a score and a scoreboard the agent never sees.** Oak's Lab → Viridian City (M1) → Route 2 (M2) → Viridian Forest (M3). Score = 0.1 per new square, 1 per level, a heal term. Real progress T = milestones reached. Three players: the score-trained agent, a reference trained on progress, a random player. Held-out starts (Bulbasaur; Viridian Center door) sealed until the patch was locked in. ▣ **route schematic** with milestones + Route 22 dead end.
  3. **Batch 1: an agent that learned nothing.** Score flat at ~1.4× random; on score, random play beat the reference. A planner with perfect memory earned ≥417 (8× the agent's best) and reached T = 5 in 16/16: covering the map *is* the route. The learner was the limit, and the pilot should have told us. ▣ **score vs progress, batch 1** (small).
  4. **The fix: give it a memory.** The policy only saw the current screen; batch 2 adds one channel marking squares visited this episode. Said plainly: designed after seeing batch 1. Check passed: best score 55.5 → 120.9 (D1).
  5. **Batch 2: score and progress went up together.** D1: score 64.6 → 112.6, T 1.05 → 1.43 (D2 similar). No bend. Two mild exploits, both attributed to the reward: off-route wandering (about 18% of reward, 8 of 10 seeds) and the Route 22 dead end (about 2%); both showed up again on the held-out starts. Why (inference): on this stretch, covering squares and making progress are the same activity; the agent covers ~27% of the planner's squares. ▣ **score vs progress, batch 2** (main figure). ▣ **where agents went** (visit heatmaps: A0 vs random).
  6. **The patch that backfired.** Cap exploration reward at the first 20 new squares per map, picked by a rule fixed in advance: removes 85.7% of the off-route reward, and 82.4% of everything else; total reward to 0.17. Result: T at 5M 0.33 mean vs 1.43 before; the agent earns less of the original score (~22) than random play (29.7); off-route share basically unchanged (0.174 vs 0.178). It shrank all exploring, not the bad kind. ▣ **where the reward came from, before vs after the patch.**
  7. **Grass grinding.** Grinding grew after the patch (not "the patch caused it": round 1 has no reference arm, and the reference also grinds in batch 2). Two of three seeds grind battles in grass, 22% of patched reward (0.2% of episodes before). Context: the level reward was untouched, so by our offline estimate its weight relative to exploring rose ~6×. The three seeds behave differently (progress-then-grind / never progresses / progresses, never grinds). ▣ **round-1 seeds as small multiples** (T per checkpoint).
  8. **Why we stopped.** The pre-set rule said patch again. We stopped (a declared deviation) because a second patch would be tested on a learner that's barely working. Held-out results repeated the dev ones. Next experiment: a selective patch, starting from the original reward.
  9. **What didn't work** (the one honest-shortcomings section): no curve here ≠ no curve anywhere; batch 2 was designed after batch 1; the reference agent was unstable (13 of 40 late checkpoints collapsed), so one headline comparison flips with a filter; a frozen checkpoint isn't the learner (resuming one that scored ~50 gave 131); noisy checkpoints and an unexplained 3M dip; round 1 is 3 seeds, one state, no reference; one segment, one proxy, one algorithm.
  10. **What I take from it.** The gap existed; the agent used it only a little. The weak link was the measuring: checkpoints, the audit, the reference. A blunt fix closed the gap by paying less for everything, and the agent moved to the next term that still paid. Links: the paper, the build-log post. Cost: ~$70 of free cloud credit.
  — **Questions for lead:** (a) is the opening's "I" OK for work the agents did (I'll say "we" for the team, "I" for Jake's choices)? (b) keep §9 one section, or split measurement issues out as their own short story beat? (c) the build-log post is still a draft: link it or not? — `content/posts/<slug>/` (not created yet)

With only 4 shades, identity never rides on shade alone: direct labels, plus a pattern or glyph per series. Every number comes from our results files; **no ROM art, sprites, tiles or screenshots**. Map shapes are drawn only from where agents actually walked (RAM x/y in our traces), never from game map data. Each SVG gets native hover tooltips and a JSON with the numbers it shows. **Planned, in build order:** (1) `route-map`: schematic of the route as blocky 'map cards' (Pallet → Route 1 → Viridian → Route 2 → Viridian Forest → Pewter → gym → Brock), milestones M1–M6 as numbered flags, the side buildings A0 learned to farm marked as off-route doors. Hand-laid schematic; labels only. (2) `score-vs-progress`: batch 2, A0 against training steps, two stacked pixel panels (proxy score P on top, real progress T below; no dual axis), with A2″ (oracle) and random as reference lines. The 'score climbs, progress doesn't keep up' picture. Optional twin for batch 1 ('learned nothing'), same layout. (3) `where-they-went`: visit heatmaps on the overworld from eval traces at 5M, three panels: A0 batch 2, A1 round 1 (patched), R0 random; each tile = share of episodes that stood on it, 4 shades, so the map appears as the agents uncover it. Map placement is computed from map-to-map transitions in our own traces, not from map files. (4) `reward-sources`: where the reward came from before and after the patch: stacked pixel bars of reward by term (explore / level / heal) and on-route vs off-route (O share), A0 under P0 vs A1 under P1 at 5M, plus R0 under each. Shows 'grass grinding grew' and 'O share didn't move'. (5) `round1-seeds`: small multiples, one 'cartridge' per A1 seed: T per checkpoint as pixel bars, with A0's line for comparison, and the level-term share as a strip under each. Game-styled sibling of Fig. 5. **D2 (optional):** after D1, a list of 4–6 candidate frames from `~/roms/runs/` (paths + one line each, kept outside the repo) for Jake to approve; nothing copied into the repo. Questions for the lead: OK to start with (1) and (2) as asked, then (3)–(5)? Is (2)'s batch-1 twin wanted?

- [researcher] **Citation re-check, loose ends closed (2026-11-10): Ng 1999 confirmed, Wiewiora 2003 wording fixed, Booth stays abstract-only and is within it** — **Ng:** I read page 1 of the PDF from Russell's Berkeley page; the abstract states that adding a potential-difference reward preserves the optimal policy and that this is necessary for invariance without further assumptions. Confirmed. **Wiewiora:** the arXiv abstract (1106.5267) attributes the invariance guarantee to Ng et al.; its own result is that potential-based shaping gives the same updates as initializing Q-values from the potential. `paper.md` had cited both for invariance; it now cites Ng for invariance and Wiewiora for the equivalence only. **Booth:** the paper's single claim ("reward functions designed by trial and error can be overfit to the learning algorithm and its hyperparameters") is what the abstract says, so no further copy was needed. That covers the ten papers `paper.md` cites for claims. Its other three citations (`pyboy`, `pret2026pokered`, `whidden2023pokemonred`) are software and repository references carrying no numeric or specific claim; they were opened when added to `sources.bib` and were not re-opened in this pass.

Status in each summary + the brief line ('H9 rule: keep/pending/escalate — why'); an escalate goes to the researcher before that run's closing eval is read. `b2_reads.py resumes RUN…` = the resumed-runs table for the batch-2 report (item 7 will include it). 8 cases tested; synced to both VMs. No batch-2 resumes so far.
- [developer] **§9 H9 standing ask (resumes) in place; note: every resume is a PARTIAL restore by design** — 2026-11-07 — A resume restores the learner exactly (weights, optimizer, step counters, torch/numpy/python RNG) but NOT the emulator/env: in-progress episodes restart from the start state and env workers are reseeded (seed·1000 + idx + 7919·resumes, so the jitter differs). So under the standing ask every resume comes to the researcher for a ruling. Now logged: each `resume_log` entry carries `restored` {weights, optimizer, step_counters, rng, emulator_state: false, env_episodes, env_seed_offset, full_restore: false, ckpt_kind (recovery 15-min vs step mark)} plus the existing resume step, stopped-at step and replayed steps; `summary-marks.md` lists resumes; the `brief` line shows 'RESUMED xN … partial restore → researcher ruling', so my health checks surface it. Synced to both VMs (code b2 logging only; running trainers unaffected). No resume has happened in batch 2 so far.
- [developer] **§9 H9 wall-clock confirmation, item by item (train.py, pokered/env.py)** — 2026-11-07 — (1) **LR / coefficients:** no schedule at all. `torch.optim.Adam(lr=2.5e-4, eps=1e-5)` is constant for the whole run; ent_coef 0.01, vf_coef 0.5, clip 0.1 are constants; nothing reads time. (2) **Checkpoints:** eval marks (`eval-<step>.pt`) and interim marks (`interim-<step>.pt`) are **step-indexed** (`global_step >= mark`). The **recovery checkpoint (`ckpt-<step>.pt`) is NOT step-indexed: it fires every 15 min of wall time** (`ckpt_minutes`). It is write-only for the run: `save_ckpt` copies `state_dict()`s and reads the RNG states with getters (`torch.get_rng_state`, `np.random.get_state`, `random.getstate`), which don't advance them, and `prune` deletes old files. When it fires can't change the computation; it only decides which file a crash would resume from, and a freeze causes no resume. (3) **Timeouts:** none in the training loop or env workers (pipes block); the only one is a 10 s `join` at shutdown. (4) **Env/emulator:** advanced by frame ticks, unthrottled (`set_emulation_speed(0)`); Red has no RTC; the game RNG reads the emulated DIV register. (5) Everything else that reads time is logging (sps, t_collect/t_learn, `time` field, `created`). So no wall-clock input reaches the learner, the env or any RNG stream; r1 stands.

- [developer] **§9 H9 (trip 1 = r1): wall-clock audit done, no wall-clock logic in the learner, env or RNG streams; e1-a pause cleared 21:39:56 UTC** — 2026-11-07 — Audit (`grep` of time/datetime/perf_counter/monotonic/signal/timeout over train.py, pokered/{env,policy,ram,detectors,walkdist,shaping}.py): (1) the only wall-clock *trigger* is train.py's recovery checkpoint every 15 min (`ckpt_minutes`); `save_ckpt` reads `torch.get_rng_state()`, `np.random.get_state()`, `random.getstate()` (getters, they don't advance any stream) and `state_dict()`s, so when it fires can't change the computation; (2) every other use is logging only: `t_collect`/`t_learn`/sps, the metrics `time` field, `created` timestamps, the default env `run_id` label, a 10 s `join` timeout at shutdown; (3) env workers block on pipes with no timeouts; (4) PyBoy runs unthrottled (`set_emulation_speed(0)`) and is advanced by frame ticks; Red has no RTC and its RNG reads the emulated DIV register (cycle count), not host time. A SIGSTOP/SIGCONT freeze changes neither thread counts nor resumes (no reseed). So the freeze can't alter training numerics: r1 stands, not r2. e1-b: clearing after its C re-check passes on ≥ 20 clean updates. Watching sps: < ≈0.88 of batch 1 or a projection over $35 → Jake before fan-out.

- [researcher] **E1 batch-1 analysis done (23 runs, dev only; held-out still sealed): A1 round 1 ON HOLD, decision for lead/Jake** — **2026-11-07** — The pipeline checklist passes on 23 runs.
  - **H0: inconclusive on D1 and D2.** A0's P is flat from the first mark (0.25M), so "P rises" fails. A2″ beats A0 on T at 3 of the 4 late marks on each state.
  - **No pre-registered hack type is present above R0 after Holm**, so nothing is eligible for round 1. The U review found no new mechanism.
  - **Main finding:** P0 is ≈ 99.7% the new-tile term, and it ranks the arms A0 (42.9) > random (30.4) > oracle A2″ (15.3), the reverse of T. That's a misspecification result, not an over-optimization curve.
